Rows: 55
Table 4
Make It Motivating: Consequence-Based Accountability
1. Q: What is the central purpose of Chapter 4, “Make It Motivating”?
A: Show answer
The chapter addresses what to do when a person understands the gap but is unwilling to take the required action. Its central argument is that effective motivation is not primarily about charisma, threats, rewards, or positional power. Instead, the person’s behavior is influenced by the consequences he or she expects from available choices. The practitioner therefore tries to make relevant natural consequences visible, accurate, and personally meaningful. The chapter places this step after describing the gap and diagnosing whether the problem is motivation or ability, and before making the task easier and moving to action.
Study instruction:
- Use the chapter model to explain where motivation fits between diagnosing a problem and agreeing on action.
Key point:
- The goal is to change the person’s understanding of consequences, not to manipulate the person into obedience.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Crucial Confrontations, Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
2. Q: How should a manager diagnose whether a performance gap is caused by motivation or ability?
A: Show answer
The response to the description of the gap should guide the next step. If the person says the task could not be completed, investigate ability barriers: knowledge, skill, resources, clarity, or other enabling conditions. If the person indicates that the task was unnecessary, not worth the effort, or displaced by something considered more important, investigate motivation. The distinction matters because solving the wrong cause produces the wrong intervention. A manager should resist immediately prescribing training, tools, rewards, or discipline before understanding the stated barrier.
Study instruction:
- Practice classifying ten workplace responses as motivation, ability, or mixed causes, and identify what additional question would test each diagnosis.
Key point:
- Diagnosis precedes intervention; the person’s response provides evidence about the path to take.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
3. Q: What does it mean to say that people are “always motivated”?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses the phrase to challenge the common description that someone simply “isn’t motivated.” A person is always choosing some behavior because he or she expects that choice to produce a preferable overall result. The practical question is therefore not whether motivation exists, but why the person prefers the current behavior over the behavior being requested. The intervention should uncover the expected consequences behind that choice.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite common statements such as “She is unmotivated” into diagnostic questions that identify what behavior the person is choosing and what consequences appear attractive.
Key point:
- Treat apparent lack of motivation as a clue about competing expected outcomes.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
4. Q: How does the chapter explain the relationship between expectations and behavior?
A: Show answer
People anticipate what an action will produce and choose among alternatives according to the expected overall consequences. A behavior may produce several outcomes simultaneously: some desirable, some undesirable, some immediate, and some delayed. If another behavior appears to offer a better overall bundle, the person may rationally choose it from his or her perspective. To influence behavior, the conversation must therefore correct incomplete or inaccurate expectations about consequences.
Study instruction:
- Take a familiar workplace behavior and map the expected positive and negative consequences of doing versus not doing it.
Key point:
- Behavior is influenced by the consequence bundle the person anticipates.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
5. Q: What is a consequence bundle?
A: Show answer
A consequence bundle is the collection of outcomes a person expects from a particular behavior. The bundle can include consequences for the individual, coworkers, customers, managers, owners, communities, or regulators. It can also contain short-term benefits and long-term costs, or immediate discomfort followed by later benefits. The chapter’s method is to help the person see the bundle more completely rather than presenting motivation as a simple reward-versus-punishment transaction.
Study instruction:
- Create a two-column consequence map for a recurring workplace behavior, separating immediate effects from delayed effects.
Key point:
- Look beyond the person’s immediate experience to the full set of affected stakeholders and time horizons.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
6. Q: Why does the chapter discourage relying on charisma to motivate people?
A: Show answer
Charisma can make an influence attempt memorable, but it does not reliably change the underlying expectations that drive ordinary behavior. Dramatic speeches are useful in entertainment but are a poor substitute for diagnosing why a person chooses one behavior over another. Everyday accountability usually depends on clear information, credible consequences, and communication rather than personal magnetism. A leader who is not naturally charismatic can still motivate effectively by helping people understand consequences.
Study instruction:
- Compare a dramatic motivational speech with a calm consequence-based discussion and identify which parts actually change the person’s information.
Key point:
- Influence does not require theatrical personality or inspirational performance.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
7. Q: Why is positional power a weak first choice for motivation?
A: Show answer
Power can produce compliance because it changes the consequences imposed by the person with authority, but it can also damage trust and create resistance. The chapter argues that people are more likely to change their understanding through expanded information and new realizations than through coercion. If authority is used too early, the other person may conclude that the relationship is adversarial and may comply only while being watched. This creates a continuing need for enforcement rather than durable ownership.
Study instruction:
- Review a workplace example where a threat produced short-term compliance and identify the relationship and monitoring costs that followed.
Key point:
- Use authority when required by legitimate policy, but do not confuse enforcement with genuine motivation.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
8. Q: What is the cost of using force too quickly?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies several costs. Force can damage relationships by moving the interaction from partnership toward policing. It can also communicate distrust or disrespect, destroy conversational safety, and trigger defensive behavior or resistance. Once people expect coercion, the person using authority may need to monitor and enforce continually. The immediate result may look efficient, but the longer-term system becomes dependent on pressure.
Study instruction:
- For a real or hypothetical confrontation, list the immediate benefit and at least three longer-term costs of using force first.
Key point:
- Short-term compliance can create long-term resistance, surveillance, and relationship damage.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
9. Q: Why can the belief that another person has bad motives make motivation harder?
A: Show answer
When people interpret another person’s behavior as evidence of a fixed character flaw, they are more likely to believe that explanation, persuasion, or patience will not work. That belief makes coercion seem necessary. The chapter warns that this dispositional interpretation can become self-reinforcing: the more harmful the behavior feels, the more malicious the person appears, and the more justified force seems. A better diagnostic stance separates the behavior from assumptions about permanent character.
Study instruction:
- Identify one situation in which you attributed a behavior to character and list alternative situational explanations that should have been tested.
Key point:
- Diagnose observable behavior and consequences before making broad judgments about character.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
10. Q: How can a person recognize that he or she is about to misuse power?
A: Show answer
The chapter treats a strong urge to reach for authority as a warning sign. Anger, impatience, thoughts such as “they will only listen if I threaten them,” or satisfaction at the prospect of imposing a penalty can indicate that the practitioner is reacting rather than diagnosing. At that point, pause and reconsider what the other person believes the consequences are. The question becomes whether information can change the expected consequence bundle before formal discipline is necessary.
Study instruction:
- Create a personal warning-light checklist of emotional and verbal cues that signal an urge to threaten or punish.
Key point:
- The impulse to use force can itself be diagnostic information about the conversation.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
11. Q: How should natural consequences be explained?
A: Show answer
Natural consequences are outcomes that arise from the behavior itself or from the surrounding system rather than consequences invented solely to punish the person. The practitioner makes these effects visible to the person, beginning with the job or task and then considering effects on relevant stakeholders. The explanation should be factual and proportionate rather than exaggerated. The purpose is to help the person understand why the requested behavior matters.
Study instruction:
- Take one missed commitment and explain its natural consequences in neutral language without adding a threat.
Key point:
- Explain consequences as information about reality, not as a disguised threat.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific sources: Crucial Accountability, Chapter 4; Crucial Learning: Accountability Course
12. Q: Which stakeholders should be considered when explaining consequences?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies multiple stakeholder groups that can be affected by a behavior, including other employees, customers, owners or shareholders, communities, and regulatory agencies. The relevant group depends on the situation. A late expense report, for example, may affect the employee, finance staff, billing processes, customers, and management. Considering stakeholders prevents the conversation from being narrowly framed around the speaker’s personal inconvenience.
Study instruction:
- For a workplace problem, draw a stakeholder map and identify one concrete consequence for each materially affected group.
Key point:
- A consequence becomes more credible when it is connected to real effects on relevant people or systems.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
13. Q: What does it mean to make the invisible visible?
A: Show answer
People sometimes know a rule or commitment but have forgotten why it exists or cannot see the downstream effects of violating it. Making the invisible visible means surfacing those consequences so the person can incorporate them into the decision. This may involve connecting an apparently small action to customer impact, coworker workload, financial results, safety, compliance, or relationship effects. The aim is better understanding, not fear.
Study instruction:
- Choose a routine task that people often dismiss as administrative and trace its downstream effects until the hidden value becomes clear.
Key point:
- Visibility of consequences can restore motivation when the original rationale has faded from attention.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Crucial Accountability, Chapter 4
14. Q: When should a manager explain consequences as part of teaching?
A: Show answer
Teaching is appropriate when the person needs to understand why a required behavior matters, particularly when the behavior is new, difficult, or connected to broader outcomes. The chapter distinguishes this from dealing with someone who already understands the rationale but repeatedly chooses another priority. Teaching should connect the requested action to consequences and help the person see its value rather than merely giving an instruction.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a short explanation for a new employee that covers both what must be done and why it matters.
Key point:
- When the knowledge gap is genuine, explain the rationale rather than treating the issue as resistance.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
15. Q: How should you respond when priorities differ?
A: Show answer
If the person knows what to do but repeatedly chooses another task, the issue may be a priority conflict rather than ignorance. The practitioner should remind the person of consequences and the reason the task belongs on the priority list. This is especially relevant when competing demands arise, when the person enjoys other work more, or when the person has learned that nobody notices if the task is omitted. The conversation should clarify what must remain non-negotiable and why.
Study instruction:
- Identify a recurring priority conflict and write three reminder questions that reconnect the task to its consequences without sounding accusatory.
Key point:
- Priority conflicts require clear consequence-based reminders, not repeated explanations of information the person already knows.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
16. Q: How should you handle someone who openly resists a requested behavior?
A: Show answer
Open resistance calls for patience and a search for consequences that the other person actually values. The practitioner should not immediately escalate to authority. Instead, explain consequences for the task, then for coworkers or customers, and continue exploring until a relevant consequence becomes meaningful. The chapter’s expense-report example shows that different people may care about different consequences; what fails to motivate one person may matter greatly to another.
Study instruction:
- Role-play a resistant employee and practice moving through several consequence categories without threatening or arguing.
Key point:
- The key is not finding the consequence that matters to you; it is finding a truthful consequence that matters to the other person.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific sources: Crucial Accountability, Chapter 4; Crucial Learning: Motivating Strangers
17. Q: What is a consequence search?
A: Show answer
A consequence search is the deliberate process of exploring different legitimate consequences until the person recognizes one that meaningfully affects his or her decision. The search can move across effects on the individual, coworkers, customers, organizational results, managers, or other stakeholders. It should not become a negotiation in which facts are invented or consequences are exaggerated. The practitioner is looking for an accurate connection that the person had not adequately considered.
Study instruction:
- Choose a difficult behavior and prepare at least five truthful consequence questions that move from personal effects to wider stakeholder effects.
Key point:
- A consequence search is exploratory rather than coercive.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Crucial Learning: Motivating Strangers
18. Q: Why is it important to avoid exaggerating consequences?
A: Show answer
The purpose of consequence-based motivation is to improve the person’s understanding of reality. Exaggeration undermines that purpose by turning information into manipulation. If the person later discovers that the claimed consequence was overstated, trust falls and future warnings become less credible. Strong accountability therefore requires consequences that are specific, evidence-based, proportionate, and connected to the actual behavior.
Study instruction:
- Review a proposed accountability statement and remove any claim that cannot be supported by observable evidence or organizational policy.
Key point:
- Credibility is a control: consequences must be real enough to withstand scrutiny.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Crucial Accountability, Chapter 4
19. Q: What is the difference between natural consequences and discipline?
A: Show answer
Natural consequences arise from the behavior and its effects; discipline involves consequences imposed through legitimate authority or organizational rules. The chapter recommends starting with natural consequences when appropriate because they preserve partnership and help the person understand the impact of the behavior. Discipline becomes appropriate when serious conduct requires immediate action, when the person continues violating agreements despite prior conversations, or when policy requires formal intervention. The distinction is important because discipline is not merely a stronger version of explanation; it changes the role of the authority figure and must follow applicable procedures.
Study instruction:
- Create a comparison table for a hypothetical workplace violation and identify which response belongs to natural-consequence discussion and which belongs to formal discipline.
Key point:
- Use natural consequences to inform and motivate; use discipline when legitimate authority and policy require imposed consequences.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Crucial Accountability, Chapter 4
20. Q: When should discipline be considered?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes discipline as a later step rather than the default opening move. It may be necessary when immediate action is required, when the person refuses to change after repeated conversations, or when a violation crosses a boundary that requires formal response. Before imposing it, the authority should understand the organization’s disciplinary process and consult appropriate specialists where necessary. Discipline should address the violation consistently and lawfully rather than express the manager’s anger.
Study instruction:
- For a sample misconduct case, identify what facts would justify escalation and what facts would require consultation before discipline.
Key point:
- Discipline is a formal intervention and should not be improvised.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Crucial Accountability, Chapter 4
21. Q: Why must managers know the mechanics of their organization’s discipline process?
A: Show answer
Disciplinary systems contain defined steps, decision rights, documentation requirements, and review mechanisms. Skipping or inventing steps can make the response inconsistent, unfair, or vulnerable to reversal. The chapter therefore advises studying the organization’s process before promising a particular penalty. In practice, the manager should know who has authority, what evidence is required, what notice is appropriate, and when HR, legal, compliance, or another specialist must be involved.
Study instruction:
- Create a pre-discipline checklist covering policy, authority, evidence, consultation, documentation, and next steps.
Key point:
- Procedural competence protects both the organization and the credibility of the manager.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Crucial Accountability, Chapter 4
22. Q: Why should a manager partner with people in authority before imposing discipline?
A: Show answer
A manager may not know the person’s complete history, applicable policy, or available disciplinary options. Consulting authorized specialists can prevent promises that later prove impossible or inappropriate. It also helps distinguish a manager’s responsibility to address the issue from the formal authority to impose a particular sanction. In sensitive cases, consultation can protect due process, consistency, confidentiality, and organizational compliance.
Study instruction:
- For a hypothetical disciplinary case, list the facts that a manager should confirm with HR or another authorized specialist before announcing a penalty.
Key point:
- Good accountability includes knowing when to stop acting alone and obtain legitimate procedural guidance.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Crucial Accountability, Chapter 4
23. Q: What tone should be used when discipline is imposed?
A: Show answer
The chapter recommends an appropriately somber tone. Discipline should be presented as a serious organizational response to a violation, not as a victory or an opportunity for revenge. The manager should focus on what must happen next, explain the basis for the response, and avoid humiliation or personal celebration. This preserves professional boundaries and reduces the risk that discipline becomes a vehicle for anger.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite an angry disciplinary statement into a factual, serious statement that focuses on the violation and required next step.
Key point:
- Discipline should communicate accountability without contempt or triumph.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Crucial Accountability, Chapter 4
24. Q: Why should the next level of consequences be explained during discipline?
A: Show answer
Explaining what will happen if the behavior is repeated reduces surprise and clarifies the path forward. It tells the person what standard must be met and what escalation may follow if the violation continues. This is not the same as threatening; it is communicating the established consequence structure. Where formal policy governs, the manager should describe only what the policy and authorized decision-makers support.
Study instruction:
- Draft a compliant escalation explanation for a hypothetical repeated violation, using only consequences supported by the stated policy.
Key point:
- Predictability makes accountability clearer and reduces disputes about what the person was told.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Crucial Accountability, Chapter 4
25. Q: Why is consistency essential in discipline?
A: Show answer
The chapter emphasizes fairness because third parties will examine whether similar violations receive comparable treatment. Selectively disciplining someone because the manager dislikes that person creates inequity and undermines legitimacy. Consistency does not necessarily mean identical outcomes in every case; relevant differences in facts, history, policy, and severity may matter. The essential principle is that differences in treatment should be grounded in legitimate factors rather than favoritism or retaliation.
Study instruction:
- Audit a hypothetical set of disciplinary decisions and identify whether differences are supported by relevant facts or by personal preference.
Key point:
- Fairness requires consistent standards and reasoned treatment of differences.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Crucial Accountability, Chapter 4
26. Q: Why does backing off after threatening discipline undermine accountability?
A: Show answer
If a manager repeatedly threatens consequences and then withdraws them without a legitimate reason, people learn that the threats are not credible. The chapter warns that wavering can create a reputation for hollow threats. A better approach is to make commitments only after understanding the applicable process, and then follow the process consistently. Credible accountability is based on predictable action rather than escalating rhetoric.
Study instruction:
- Identify a situation in which an unfulfilled threat weakened authority and propose a more disciplined approach to setting expectations.
Key point:
- Do not threaten a consequence you are not prepared and authorized to implement.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Crucial Accountability, Chapter 4
27. Q: What should you do when you cannot or should not impose discipline?
A: Show answer
The chapter proposes a candid coping strategy. If the other person is unlikely to change and the practitioner lacks appropriate authority to impose a consequence, the practitioner can change how he or she interacts with the person while openly explaining that adjustment. Examples include avoiding critical-path assignments for an unreliable partner or withdrawing from an emotionally explosive interaction until the person is calmer. The important point is that the coping strategy is not secretly designed to conceal the problem; it is communicated so the other person can understand the consequence and choose whether to change.
Study instruction:
- Design a transparent coping strategy for a situation where you have influence but no formal authority to compel change.
Key point:
- When you cannot control another person, you can still control your own boundaries, choices, and exposure to the consequences.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Crucial Accountability, Chapter 4
28. Q: How can a work-around unintentionally reinforce bad behavior?
A: Show answer
A work-around can remove the consequences that would otherwise reveal the cost of the behavior. If someone repeatedly fails to return calls and another employee secretly does the work, the unreliable person may never experience the operational impact. The organization has solved the immediate problem while preserving the underlying behavior. Over time, these hidden accommodations can make dysfunction stable. The chapter calls attention to the possibility that the people who complain about a behavior may also be helping it persist.
Study instruction:
- Map a recurring workplace problem and identify every hidden workaround that may be shielding the person from natural consequences.
Key point:
- A workaround can solve today’s symptom while preserving tomorrow’s problem.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Crucial Accountability, Chapter 4
29. Q: What does the chapter mean by saying that we can become “conspirators” in bad behavior?
A: Show answer
The term describes situations in which people avoid confronting a problem and then create accommodations that allow it to continue. The original actor may remain unaware of the consequences, while others absorb the cost, compensate for the behavior, or redesign work around it. This does not mean responsibility for the bad behavior shifts to the people who accommodate it. It means that accountability systems can unintentionally reinforce behavior when consequences are continually hidden.
Study instruction:
- Identify one organizational behavior that persists because colleagues compensate for it, and distinguish the original responsibility from the enabling workaround.
Key point:
- Accountability requires examining the system of responses around a behavior, not only the actor.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Crucial Accountability, Chapter 4
30. Q: How should natural consequences be used without becoming manipulative?
A: Show answer
The practitioner should describe consequences that are real, relevant, and proportionate, then allow the other person to evaluate them. Manipulation would involve inventing consequences, selectively withholding material facts, exaggerating harm, or engineering pain solely to force compliance. The chapter’s approach is closer to informed choice: make the existing consequence bundle visible and let the person decide. If formal rules require discipline, that should be explained as a separate authority-based process.
Study instruction:
- Review a proposed influence conversation and label each statement as factual consequence, interpretation, threat, or manipulation.
Key point:
- Ethical consequence-based motivation informs choice; it does not manufacture fear.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific sources: Crucial Accountability, Chapter 4; Crucial Learning: Accountability Course
31. Q: How should a manager respond when the person says, “I already know the consequences”?
A: Show answer
Treat that statement as useful diagnostic information rather than as a challenge to authority. Ask what the person believes the consequences are, which ones matter, and why the requested behavior still loses the priority contest. The person may indeed understand the consequences but value immediate benefits more highly, believe the consequences are unlikely, or have conflicting incentives. The conversation should therefore move from explanation toward understanding the competing consequence bundle.
Study instruction:
- Prepare three follow-up questions that distinguish ignorance of consequences from disagreement about their importance or likelihood.
Key point:
- Knowing a consequence exists is not the same as valuing it enough to change behavior.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
32. Q: How can short-term and long-term consequences compete?
A: Show answer
People may choose a behavior because its immediate consequence is attractive even when its long-term consequence is harmful. Avoiding an uncomfortable conversation, skipping a tedious control, or delaying a report can reduce immediate effort while increasing later risk. The chapter recommends making the broader bundle visible, including future effects that may currently be discounted. A useful conversation connects the short-term choice with the longer-term outcome without exaggerating certainty.
Study instruction:
- Take a delayed-cost behavior and identify the immediate benefit that competes with the longer-term benefit of doing the right thing.
Key point:
- Motivation often fails because immediate consequences are more visible than delayed consequences.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
33. Q: How can the consequence of a behavior affect coworkers even when the actor does not notice it?
A: Show answer
A person may see only his or her own task while missing the work created for others. A late report can trigger rework, delayed billing, overtime, missed deadlines, or additional follow-up. The chapter recommends widening the consequence discussion to other stakeholders when the person does not respond to consequences affecting the task itself. The explanation should focus on actual operational links rather than guilt.
Study instruction:
- Trace a delayed task through at least three downstream processes and identify where additional work is created.
Key point:
- Unseen workload on others is often a meaningful part of the consequence bundle.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
34. Q: How can customer consequences be used appropriately?
A: Show answer
Customer effects can be highly motivating when the behavior directly affects service, quality, reliability, safety, or trust. The practitioner should explain the actual customer impact, such as delays, inaccurate service, missed commitments, or reduced confidence. The goal is not to shame the employee with abstract claims about customers but to connect the behavior to a concrete service outcome. Customer consequences should be used only when the causal connection is genuine.
Study instruction:
- Take a quality problem and explain its customer consequences in observable terms, avoiding inflated statements.
Key point:
- Customer impact can make organizational purpose tangible when the connection is real.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
35. Q: How can financial consequences be discussed without turning the conversation into a threat?
A: Show answer
Financial consequences should be presented as operational facts: revenue delayed, costs increased, billing affected, resources wasted, or financial commitments placed at risk. The manager should explain the causal chain and distinguish known amounts from estimates. Saying “this will cost you your job” is a threat; explaining that repeated late reporting delays billing and increases administrative cost is information about the system. Formal financial penalties, if any, should be described according to policy rather than invented during the conversation.
Study instruction:
- Build a simple causal chain from a behavior to a financial effect and mark which figures are verified and which are estimates.
Key point:
- Financial consequences motivate most effectively when they are concrete, credible, and connected to the process.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
36. Q: What should happen if a person remains resistant after several natural consequences are explained?
A: Show answer
First confirm that the person understands the consequences and that the issue is genuinely motivational rather than an ability barrier or another problem. If the person still refuses to act and the behavior violates a clear expectation, the conversation may move toward formal consequences or discipline when authorized. The escalation should be deliberate, policy-based, and proportionate. The practitioner should not keep repeating the same explanation indefinitely or escalate emotionally simply because the first attempts failed.
Study instruction:
- Create an escalation decision tree that distinguishes continued motivation problems, ability barriers, unclear expectations, and conduct requiring formal action.
Key point:
- Escalation should follow diagnosis and policy, not frustration.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Crucial Accountability, Chapter 4
37. Q: How should motivation and ability be handled when both are present?
A: Show answer
The chapter warns against assuming a single cause. A person may understand why a task matters but lack the skill or resources to perform it, or may have the ability but insufficient motivation. In mixed cases, address both dimensions: make the consequences clear while also removing genuine barriers. Treating a mixed problem as purely motivational can lead to unfair pressure; treating it as purely an ability problem can provide resources without changing the priority.
Study instruction:
- Use a case with both motivation and ability barriers and design a two-part intervention that addresses each cause separately.
Key point:
- Motivation and ability are distinct diagnostic dimensions that may require different interventions.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
38. Q: How should the practitioner maintain safety while discussing consequences?
A: Show answer
The chapter’s examples connect motivation to the broader Crucial Confrontation model, where safety remains active throughout the conversation. Consequences should be described without sarcasm, humiliation, personal attacks, or threats. If the other person interprets a natural consequence as blackmail, the practitioner should clarify intent and restore the purpose of the conversation. Safety does not mean avoiding difficult facts; it means communicating them in a way that preserves respect and a legitimate mutual purpose.
Study instruction:
- Practice converting a threatening-sounding consequence statement into a respectful explanation of impact and purpose.
Key point:
- Safety and accountability are complementary: difficult information can be direct without becoming hostile.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
39. Q: What does the Gary-and-Kali case illustrate about motivation?
A: Show answer
The marriage case illustrates that the opening moments of a difficult conversation can determine whether the other person will engage. Kali makes the problem visible, expresses a constructive purpose, and explains the likely consequences of continued silence. When Gary interprets the statement as a threat, she steps out of the content, clarifies that she is not attacking him, acknowledges her own contribution to the pattern, and restores safety. Only after safety is restored can they jointly explore the problem.
Study instruction:
- Study the dialogue sequence and identify each move that increases safety, communicates consequences, or clarifies mutual purpose.
Key point:
- Motivating someone to participate may begin with making the conversation itself safe enough to enter.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
40. Q: Why did Kali avoid making her consequence statement sound like a threat?
A: Show answer
Kali wanted Gary to understand that continued avoidance could damage the marriage, but she did not want him to interpret that information as emotional blackmail. When he asked whether she was threatening him, she clarified her intent and explained that she wanted an open discussion. This distinction is central to consequence-based motivation: a natural consequence can be serious without being used as a weapon.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite a consequence statement that could easily be heard as a threat, adding a clear statement of purpose and respect.
Key point:
- A consequence can be candid and serious while remaining distinct from coercion.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
41. Q: How can a leader use consequences to reinforce organizational purpose?
A: Show answer
The leader can connect the requested behavior to outcomes that the organization actually exists to achieve, such as customer service, safety, quality, reliability, compliance, or responsible use of resources. This helps the person see the task as part of a larger system rather than as a personal preference of the manager. The connection must be specific: the leader should explain how the behavior contributes to the outcome and what happens when it is omitted.
Study instruction:
- Select a routine control or process and explain its relationship to the organization’s larger purpose in three steps.
Key point:
- Purpose becomes motivating when people can see the causal connection between their behavior and meaningful outcomes.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific sources: Crucial Learning: Accountability Course; Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
42. Q: How can a manager distinguish a real priority conflict from deliberate neglect?
A: Show answer
Both can look similar because the task is not completed. The difference lies in the person’s decision process and the surrounding expectations. A genuine priority conflict may involve competing urgent demands, unclear sequencing, or changed circumstances. Deliberate neglect may involve knowing the expectation and repeatedly deciding that it does not matter because the consequences appear low or hidden. The manager should investigate rather than assume intent, then clarify priorities and consequences.
Study instruction:
- Interview a hypothetical employee about a missed deadline and identify questions that distinguish overload, ambiguity, and deliberate deprioritization.
Key point:
- Do not infer motive from the outcome alone; investigate the decision conditions.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
43. Q: What common mistake occurs when a manager explains consequences only from the manager’s perspective?
A: Show answer
The manager may explain why the problem is frustrating to the manager without identifying why it matters to the other person. That can fail because motivation depends on the other person’s expected outcomes. A better approach broadens the search to consequences that the person values, while remaining truthful. The manager’s inconvenience is relevant, but it is only one component of the overall consequence bundle.
Study instruction:
- Take a weak accountability statement centered on the manager’s frustration and rewrite it using consequences relevant to the employee or stakeholders.
Key point:
- Effective motivation requires understanding the other person’s decision frame, not merely expressing your own frustration.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
44. Q: What common mistake occurs when a manager assumes that punishment is the only alternative to silence?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies this as a false dichotomy. The manager may believe the only options are to tolerate the behavior or use threats, leading either to avoidance or coercion. The consequence-based approach provides a third path: explain the real effects of the behavior, explore what matters to the person, and preserve the relationship while increasing accountability. Formal discipline remains available when required, but it is not the only influence mechanism.
Study instruction:
- Analyze a situation where a manager has chosen between silence and punishment and design a third option based on natural consequences.
Key point:
- Avoidance and coercion are not the only choices; informed accountability provides another path.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
45. Q: How can leaders avoid turning accountability into a police-state relationship?
A: Show answer
Leaders should avoid making surveillance and punishment the normal mechanism for getting work done. They can preserve partnership by clarifying expectations, discussing consequences, diagnosing barriers, and reserving formal authority for cases that genuinely require it. The objective is for people to understand and own the reasons for the behavior rather than comply only when watched. When enforcement is necessary, it should be proportionate and consistent rather than the default leadership style.
Study instruction:
- Assess a team’s current accountability system and identify which controls rely on understanding versus surveillance or punishment.
Key point:
- Healthy accountability reduces the need for constant policing by improving clarity, motivation, and ownership.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
46. Q: How can managers use consequence-based motivation with high performers?
A: Show answer
The chapter notes that the skills can help good performers become great. High performers may still neglect an important behavior when its purpose is unclear, when a competing priority appears more valuable, or when a consequence has become invisible through routine. Rather than assuming strong performance eliminates the need for motivation, leaders can use consequence discussions to connect high standards with customer, team, quality, or organizational outcomes.
Study instruction:
- Choose one behavior of a strong performer that could be improved and identify a consequence that would make the improvement meaningful without micromanaging.
Key point:
- Even capable people can reprioritize behavior when expected consequences change.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Crucial Learning: Accountability Course
47. Q: What should a manager do when the person values the short-term benefit of noncompliance more than the long-term benefit of compliance?
A: Show answer
First acknowledge the trade-off rather than pretending the short-term benefit does not exist. Then clarify the complete consequence bundle, including the delayed costs and benefits. If the behavior still violates a clear requirement, the manager should explain the expectation and, where appropriate, the formal consequences of continued noncompliance. The objective is not to pretend that every requested behavior is immediately pleasant, but to make the real trade-off explicit.
Study instruction:
- Take a recurring shortcut and identify its immediate benefit, delayed cost, and the legitimate consequence if the shortcut violates a requirement.
Key point:
- Good accountability makes trade-offs visible instead of pretending they do not exist.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
48. Q: How should a manager handle an employee who says, “Nobody else does this”?
A: Show answer
Do not immediately argue about what everyone else does. Clarify the actual expectation, the reason it exists, and the consequences of following or ignoring it. If there is evidence that the standard is inconsistently applied, that is a management problem that should be addressed separately. If the employee is simply using others’ behavior to justify noncompliance, the manager can return to the individual expectation and its consequences.
Study instruction:
- Practice responding to the claim without defensiveness, and identify what evidence would be needed to investigate whether the standard is actually inconsistent.
Key point:
- Peer behavior can be relevant evidence, but it does not automatically determine the individual’s obligation.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
49. Q: How should consequences be documented in a professional accountability process?
A: Show answer
Document the expectation, observed gap, relevant facts, stated impact, agreed action, and any formal consequence that policy requires. Avoid recording speculation about personality or motive as fact. Where discipline is involved, follow the organization’s documentation rules and confidentiality requirements. Good records support consistency, follow-up, and later review while keeping the focus on behavior and evidence.
Study instruction:
- Create a documentation template with fields for expectation, behavior, evidence, consequence, response, commitment, and follow-up.
Key point:
- Document observable facts and legitimate decisions, not character judgments.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Crucial Accountability, Chapter 4
50. Q: How can consequence-based motivation support risk management?
A: Show answer
Many risks persist because warning signs or downstream effects are invisible to the person making the immediate decision. Making consequences visible can connect a small behavior to safety, financial, operational, reputational, compliance, or customer risks. The technique complements formal risk controls; it does not replace them. When a consequence is serious enough to require mandatory escalation, the appropriate formal process should still be followed.
Study instruction:
- Take a control weakness and map the behavior, immediate effect, downstream risk, and affected stakeholders.
Key point:
- Motivation can strengthen risk awareness, but formal risk controls remain necessary.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Crucial Learning: Accountability Course
51. Q: How should consequence-based motivation be adapted to regulated or safety-critical work?
A: Show answer
The natural-consequence discussion remains useful for understanding why the behavior matters, but it cannot substitute for mandatory requirements. In safety, legal, safeguarding, financial-control, or regulatory contexts, the practitioner should identify the required standard, explain real consequences, and follow the mandated escalation or reporting process. If immediate protective action is required, authority may need to be used promptly. Motivation should never become an excuse to delay a required intervention.
Study instruction:
- Take a safety-critical scenario and separate the motivational conversation from the mandatory protective or reporting actions.
Key point:
- Where rules require action, compliance duties take precedence over conversational preference.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
52. Q: How can a leader avoid using shame when explaining consequences?
A: Show answer
Focus on behavior, impact, and choice rather than identity. “This delay caused the customer to wait another day” describes an effect; “You are selfish and irresponsible” attacks character. Shame may create defensiveness and concealment rather than learning. The chapter’s consequence-based approach supports respectful explanation of outcomes while preserving the possibility that the person can choose differently.
Study instruction:
- Convert five character-based statements into behavior-and-impact statements.
Key point:
- Accountability is stronger when it targets behavior and consequences rather than personal worth.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
53. Q: What should happen if the consequence search reveals that the requested behavior is not actually worthwhile?
A: Show answer
The practitioner should be willing to reconsider the expectation. The chapter’s approach is not about winning compliance at all costs. If discussion reveals that the task has little value, that priorities conflict legitimately, or that the expected outcome no longer justifies the burden, the correct response may be to change the task or expectation. Effective accountability includes the possibility that the original demand was wrong or outdated.
Study instruction:
- Use a real or hypothetical routine task and test whether its expected benefits justify its cost.
Key point:
- A good consequence search can improve the expectation itself, not merely compliance with it.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
54. Q: How should a manager finish a motivation conversation?
A: Show answer
Once the person agrees to act, the conversation should not end with vague goodwill. The chapter directs the practitioner to finish well by deciding who will do what and by when, and by setting a follow-up time. This converts motivation into an observable commitment. Follow-up belongs to the later accountability process, but the initial conversation should establish the conditions needed for that process.
Study instruction:
- Write a closing sequence that captures owner, action, deadline, and follow-up date.
Key point:
- Motivation creates willingness; clear commitments and follow-up turn willingness into action.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
55. Q: What is the relationship between making it motivating and making it easy?
A: Show answer
The chapter separates motivation from ability. A person may want to perform the behavior but be unable because of skill, resources, clarity, or other barriers. In that case, making the consequences visible is insufficient; the next step is to diagnose and address ability. Conversely, making a task easier will not solve a problem where the person deliberately chooses not to do it. The two interventions complement one another but should not be confused.
Study instruction:
- For three cases, identify whether the next intervention should primarily address motivation, ability, or both.
Key point:
- Do not solve an ability problem with pressure or a motivation problem with tools alone.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
56. Q: How does the chapter fit into the broader Crucial Confrontation model?
A: Show answer
The model moves from preparing oneself and choosing what to address, to confronting with safety, to moving toward action. Chapter 4 sits in the “Confront with Safety” portion after describing the gap. The sequence is diagnostic: first identify the problem, then determine whether motivation or ability is contributing, then make the relevant dimension actionable. The chapter closes by linking motivation to a plan and follow-up, which are developed further later in the book.
Study instruction:
- Draw the full model and highlight where “Make It Motivating” begins, what it depends on, and what follows it.
Key point:
- Motivation is one stage in a larger accountability process, not a standalone technique.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific sources: Crucial Confrontations, Chapter 4; Crucial Confrontations: Contents
57. Q: How can a leader use the chapter’s approach with someone who has little respect for the leader’s authority?
A: Show answer
The chapter’s central premise makes formal power less decisive. The leader can diagnose the behavior, identify the relevant consequence bundle, explain effects on stakeholders, and search for consequences that matter to the person. The leader should not pretend to have authority that does not exist. If formal escalation is available, it should be used through legitimate channels when necessary; otherwise, the leader can use candid influence and transparent boundaries.
Study instruction:
- Design an influence conversation with a peer or senior colleague where you have limited authority.
Key point:
- Influence can work through information and consequences even when positional power is weak.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific sources: Crucial Learning: Motivating Strangers; Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
58. Q: How can the chapter’s principles be applied to cross-functional work?
A: Show answer
Cross-functional teams often have competing priorities and different views of who bears the cost of a delay. A consequence-based conversation can make dependencies visible: one team’s missed input may delay another team’s reporting, customer service, procurement, or delivery. The leader should map the actual dependency and ask what outcome matters to the person responsible for the upstream task. This can produce alignment without relying on hierarchy.
Study instruction:
- Choose a cross-functional dependency and map the upstream behavior to downstream consequences across teams.
Key point:
- Cross-functional motivation improves when dependencies and shared outcomes are visible.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Crucial Learning: Improve Alignment Across Teams
59. Q: How can consequence-based motivation be used in NGO or humanitarian operations?
A: Show answer
The same logic can apply to operational behaviors such as timely reporting, safeguarding escalation, procurement compliance, partner documentation, field reporting, or resource stewardship. A finance or program manager can explain how a missed control affects donor reporting, project delivery, audit readiness, beneficiary service, or other legitimate stakeholders. However, formal donor, safeguarding, financial, and legal requirements remain mandatory and should be followed through the organization’s established procedures.
Study instruction:
- Choose an NGO control and map the behavior to project, donor, beneficiary, and organizational consequences without inventing policy requirements.
Key point:
- Make consequences visible while keeping formal compliance duties separate from motivational technique.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Crucial Accountability, Chapter 4
60. Q: What ethical boundary should govern the use of consequences?
A: Show answer
The practitioner should communicate truthful, relevant consequences and preserve the other person’s dignity and legitimate choice. The technique becomes unethical when it relies on deception, humiliation, fabricated risks, disproportionate pressure, retaliation, or exploitation of irrelevant vulnerabilities. Formal authority should be exercised only within legitimate policy and law. The chapter’s preference for natural consequences is valuable partly because it reduces the temptation to manufacture pain simply to force behavior.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate a proposed consequence statement against four tests: truth, relevance, proportionality, and legitimacy.
Key point:
- Ethical motivation makes reality clearer; it does not create artificial suffering for compliance.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
61. Q: How should a manager respond when the person becomes defensive while consequences are being discussed?
A: Show answer
The manager should avoid escalating the content battle. If the person interprets the consequence explanation as a threat or personal attack, step out of the content, clarify the purpose, and restore safety. Once the person is willing to engage, return to the factual consequence and the shared problem. This preserves the diagnostic purpose of the conversation instead of turning the discussion into an argument about tone or intent.
Study instruction:
- Practice a three-step response to defensiveness: pause the content, clarify purpose, and return to the issue.
Key point:
- When safety drops, repair the conversation before continuing the consequence search.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
62. Q: How can a manager test whether the consequence discussion actually changed understanding?
A: Show answer
Ask the person to explain the situation in his or her own words: what happens if the behavior continues, who is affected, and why the requested action matters. This is more reliable than asking, “Do you understand?” because a quick yes may hide disagreement or confusion. The manager should also confirm the intended action and any remaining barriers. Follow-up then provides evidence of whether the new understanding translated into behavior.
Study instruction:
- Create a short teach-back checklist for accountability conversations.
Key point:
- Understanding is demonstrated through accurate explanation and subsequent action, not merely verbal agreement.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
63. Q: How should leaders handle repeated violations when the natural consequence has already been made clear?
A: Show answer
At that point, repeating the same explanation may have diminishing value. Reassess whether the issue is actually motivation, whether the consequence is credible, whether priorities or ability have changed, and whether a formal response is now required. If the behavior violates a documented standard and prior conversations have failed, follow the applicable disciplinary or escalation process. The manager should also examine whether hidden workarounds have continued to shield the person from consequences.
Study instruction:
- Conduct a second-level diagnosis before escalating: expectation, motivation, ability, credibility of consequences, and hidden workarounds.
Key point:
- Repeated failure after clear understanding may require a different intervention, not simply a louder explanation.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
64. Q: What are the most important mistakes to avoid when trying to motivate someone?
A: Show answer
Major mistakes include assuming the person is simply unmotivated, attributing bad character without diagnosis, relying on charisma, using power too early, exaggerating consequences, focusing only on the manager’s inconvenience, ignoring competing priorities, hiding the downstream effects of behavior, creating secret workarounds, improvising discipline, threatening consequences that will not be enforced, and failing to finish with clear commitments. Each mistake weakens either diagnosis, credibility, safety, or follow-through.
Study instruction:
- Audit a recent accountability conversation against this mistake list and identify one preventive change for future discussions.
Key point:
- Effective motivation depends as much on avoiding predictable errors as on applying a technique.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
65. Q: How should a practitioner know whether to continue searching for natural consequences or move to discipline?
A: Show answer
Continue the natural-consequence approach when the person is engaging, the behavior is appropriate for conversational influence, and additional understanding may change the choice. Move toward formal escalation when the behavior is serious, immediate action is required, repeated commitments are violated, or organizational policy mandates a formal response. The transition should be based on facts and authorized process rather than emotional exhaustion. Consultation may be required before deciding the exact disciplinary step.
Study instruction:
- Build an escalation matrix using severity, urgency, repetition, policy requirements, and evidence of prior understanding.
Key point:
- The boundary between influence and discipline is determined by risk, authority, and policy as well as motivation.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Crucial Accountability, Chapter 4
66. Q: What does it mean to finish well after motivation has been established?
A: Show answer
Finishing well means translating the conversation into a concrete plan. The parties decide who will do what and by when, and they set a follow-up point. This prevents the common failure in which a person agrees during a conversation but no operational commitment exists afterward. The chapter treats follow-up as the bridge from conversational agreement to sustained behavior.
Study instruction:
- Use a case study to write a complete closing statement with owner, action, deadline, and follow-up.
Key point:
- A motivated agreement still needs operational structure to become reliable action.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific source: Chapter 4: Make It Motivating
67. Q: What is the practical lesson of Chapter 4 for leaders?
A: Show answer
The practical lesson is to replace the reflex to control people with disciplined diagnosis and consequence-based communication. When someone chooses not to act, ask what outcome makes the current behavior attractive. Make relevant consequences visible, search for consequences the person values, preserve safety, use formal power only when appropriate, and avoid hidden workarounds that erase the costs of bad behavior. Then finish with clear commitments and follow-up. The chapter presents motivation as a structured influence problem rather than a personality contest.
Study instruction:
- Write a one-page personal playbook for handling a motivation problem using the chapter’s sequence.
Key point:
- The leader’s task is to clarify consequences, preserve safety, and create conditions for responsible choice.
Source links:
- Chapter-specific sources: Crucial Confrontations, Chapter 4; Crucial Learning: Accountability Course

5. Q: Why is it useful to involve the person closest to the problem?
A: Show answer
We hope you wouldn’t actually do this. Being the steely-eyed smart person you are, you would note that Kyle was motivated to do the job. Piling on more reasons for doing something he wasn’t able to do in the first place would be the wrong cure. Indeed, it would be cruel. Kyle needs help removing the barriers he’s facing, not a kick in the pants, and so that’s where we’ll turn. What does it take to help others remove any and all barriers they face? Or fill out expense reports? Or write a term paper?

19. Q: Why should leaders ask whether others have the same problem?
A: Show answer
Believe it or not, sometimes people purposely hide the genuine source of a problem. If they fear that they’ll get in trouble for not being able or not wanting to do what’s been asked, they may stretch the truth to avoid new problems. For example, an attending physician asks a medical student to insert an intravenous line into the chest of a 75-year-old patient. The student isn’t quite sure how to do it, but when the doctor is called away to work on a cardiac arrest, the student says nothing. Instead, he attempts to insert the line and punctures the sac around the woman’s lung, and the patient later dies of related complications. A woman dies because the student is uncomfortable saying that he just might be unable to do what he’s been asked. (This actually happened.)

20. Q: How should root causes be explored?
A: Show answer
Perhaps the most common ability problem people try to hide is their illiteracy (23 percent of the population is illiterate). Employees fear they’ll lose their jobs if they admit that they can’t read or do basic math. You ask, “John, how come you didn’t set up the new equipment?” John couldn’t read the directions, tried his best, and failed. He thinks he’ll be fired if you find out that he can’t read, and so he answers, “I hate doing that kind of stuff. It has all those fancy numbers and charts and things—not that I couldn’t do it if I wanted to.”

41. Q: How should an ability solution be followed up?

In any case, start ability discussions with a simple question: “You’ve been working on the problem. What do you think needs to be done?” Ask them for their ideas. - Diagnose the condition that prevents successful performance before prescribing a solution.
- Preserve accountability while removing genuine barriers.

46. Q: How can the approach support effective accountability?

We’re not suggesting that you manipulate people into thinking that your ideas are theirs. Involving others is not a cheap trick. We’re simply proposing that other people do have ideas, that getting them out in the open is to everyone’s advantage, and that when people are involved in the entire thought process, they see why things need to be done a certain way and are motivated to do it that way.
